It’s embarrassing because none of it is huge by itself, but it’s the kind of stuff I usually keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think so, not really. I get frustrated with myself, definitely, like when I realize I forgot something again and then I’m annoyed for the rest of the afternoon. But sad or down… I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more like, you know when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there, except it’s with actual things I needed to do. So I feel kind of stupid sometimes, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… overwhelmed maybe in the normal way? Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. That was, what, about 15 months ago? Around then I started noticing I couldn’t just sort of keep things in my head the way I used to.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… learning new information? I don’t know. I guess it depends what kind of information you mean.

Like if someone explains something to me and I’m actually paying attention, I can usually get it. But sometimes if it’s a lot of steps, I’ll write it down and then later I look at my notes and think, “Why did I write it like that?” That happened with this new phone system at work—well, not new-new, but they changed one part of it, and everyone was complaining about it anyway, so maybe that wasn’t just me.

I wouldn’t say I can’t learn things, though. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. But I haven’t really noticed some big problem with learning, if that’s what you mean.

Yeah… I think so. It’s not that I can never focus, but I get pulled off really easily.

Like at the office, if a phone rings or people are talking nearby, suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was typing. Or in class, someone shifts around or whispers and I lose my place. I get frustrated with myself, definitely, like when I realize I forgot something again and then I’m annoyed for the rest of the afternoon. But sad or down… I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more like, you know when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there, except it’s with actual things I needed to do. So I feel kind of stupid sometimes, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… overwhelmed maybe in the normal way? Like when there are too many little things stacked up and I’m trying to remember them all. But anxious, like panic or something, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be going through my day and then remember something I forgot, and then I get annoyed with myself. And then I’ll start thinking about how I need a better system—my sister has this whole color-coded calendar thing, which I always think I should try, but then I buy the planner and don’t really use it. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, really. Like, I still like the same things when I actually sit down to do them. Sometimes I don’t get around to them, but that’s kind of normal for me, I guess.\n\nI was going to say, I have this book I started and it’s been sitting on my nightstand forever, but that happens with books all the time—I’ll buy one because someone at work says it’s good, and then I read three chapters and forget about it. So I don’t know if that’s “loss of interest” exactly. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. But adding classes after work kind of made everything feel like there were too many tabs open. I’d be trying to remember work stuff, class stuff, and then normal home things, and I’d lose track more often. So… yeah, I guess compared to a year ago, it’s been more noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: Um… learning new information? I don’t know. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.

Or I had this registration form that I needed to turn in, and it just sat in my bag for days—I kept seeing it and thinking, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then somehow it was still there.\n\nAnd with errands, I’ll be driving home and then realize later I completely forgot to stop where I was supposed to. It’s embarrassing because none of it is huge by itself, but it’s the kind of stuff I usually keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think so, not really. I get frustrated with myself, definitely, like when I realize I forgot something again and then I’m annoyed for the rest of the afternoon. But sad or down… I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more like, you know when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there, except it’s with actual things I needed to do. So I feel kind of stupid sometimes, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… overwhelmed maybe in the normal way? Like when there are too many little things stacked up and I’m trying to remember them all. But anxious, like panic or something, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be going through my day and then remember something I forgot, and then I get annoyed with myself. And then I’ll start thinking about how I need a better system—my sister has this whole color-coded calendar thing, which I always think I should try, but then I buy the planner and don’t really use it. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, really. Like, I still like the same things when I actually sit down to do them. Sometimes I don’t get around to them, but that’s kind of normal for me, I guess.\n\nI was going to say, I have this book I started and it’s been sitting on my nightstand forever, but that happens with books all the time—I’ll buy one because someone at work says it’s good, and then I read three chapters and forget about it. So I don’t know if that’s “loss of interest” exactly. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. That was, what, about 15 months ago? Around then I started noticing I couldn’t just sort of keep things in my head the way I used to.\n\nI mean, I was already forgetful before, so it’s not brand new. But adding classes after work kind of made everything feel like there were too many tabs open. I’d be trying to remember work stuff, class stuff, and then normal home things, and I’d lose track more often. So… yeah, I guess compared to a year ago, it’s been more noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: Um… learning new information? I don’t know. I guess it depends what kind of information you mean.\n\nLike if someone explains something to me and I’m actually paying attention, I can usually get it. But sometimes if it’s a lot of steps, I’ll write it down and then later I look at my notes and think, “Why did I write it like that?” That happened with this new phone system at work—well, not new-new, but they changed one part of it, and everyone was complaining about it anyway, so maybe that wasn’t just me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I can’t learn things, though. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. But I haven’t really noticed some big problem with learning, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not that I can never focus, but I get pulled off really easily.\n\nLike at the office, if a phone rings or people are talking nearby, suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was typing. Or in class, someone shifts around or whispers and I lose my place. Even just a random thought, like “Did I leave that thing out at home?” can take me completely away from what I was doing. Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.\n\nGuilt… maybe if it affects someone else, I’ll feel bad for a bit. Like if I forget to pass along a message at work—well, I try not to, because I keep sticky notes everywhere, which drives my coworker a little crazy because they end up on the edge of my monitor and then I forget which ones are old. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having big feelings of worthlessness or guilt like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean… significant decline, I’m not sure I’d say that. I’m still getting by day to day. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.

It’s more like, you know when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there, except it’s with actual things I needed to do. So I feel kind of stupid sometimes, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… overwhelmed maybe in the normal way? Like when there are too many little things stacked up and I’m trying to remember them all. But anxious, like panic or something, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be going through my day and then remember something I forgot, and then I get annoyed with myself. And then I’ll start thinking about how I need a better system—my sister has this whole color-coded calendar thing, which I always think I should try, but then I buy the planner and don’t really use it. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, really. Like, I still like the same things when I actually sit down to do them. Sometimes I don’t get around to them, but that’s kind of normal for me, I guess.\n\nI was going to say, I have this book I started and it’s been sitting on my nightstand forever, but that happens with books all the time—I’ll buy one because someone at work says it’s good, and then I read three chapters and forget about it. So I don’t know if that’s “loss of interest” exactly. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. That was, what, about 15 months ago? Around then I started noticing I couldn’t just sort of keep things in my head the way I used to.\n\nI mean, I was already forgetful before, so it’s not brand new. But adding classes after work kind of made everything feel like there were too many tabs open. I’d be trying to remember work stuff, class stuff, and then normal home things, and I’d lose track more often. So… yeah, I guess compared to a year ago, it’s been more noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: Um… learning new information? I don’t know. I guess it depends what kind of information you mean.\n\nLike if someone explains something to me and I’m actually paying attention, I can usually get it. But sometimes if it’s a lot of steps, I’ll write it down and then later I look at my notes and think, “Why did I write it like that?” That happened with this new phone system at work—well, not new-new, but they changed one part of it, and everyone was complaining about it anyway, so maybe that wasn’t just me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I can’t learn things, though. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. But I haven’t really noticed some big problem with learning, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not that I can never focus, but I get pulled off really easily.\n\nLike at the office, if a phone rings or people are talking nearby, suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was typing. Or in class, someone shifts around or whispers and I lose my place. Even just a random thought, like “Did I leave that thing out at home?” can take me completely away from what I was doing. Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.\n\nGuilt… maybe if it affects someone else, I’ll feel bad for a bit. Like if I forget to pass along a message at work—well, I try not to, because I keep sticky notes everywhere, which drives my coworker a little crazy because they end up on the edge of my monitor and then I forget which ones are old. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having big feelings of worthlessness or guilt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or make decisions compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I mean… significant decline, I’m not sure I’d say that. I’m still getting by day to day. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.\n\nBut compared to a few years ago… or maybe I’m just noticing it more now because I’m trying to move up at work—I keep thinking about this Office Manager role that might open up, and then I’ll remember, like, oh, I missed following up on that one email, or I didn’t circle back with someone when I said I would. And with school stuff too, I’ve missed deadlines enough times that it kind of scares me.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s enough that I worry people are going to see me as unreliable, even though I’m really trying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It sounds little, but it’s been happening enough that I thought maybe I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you also forgetting things related to your work or studies?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. It’s more these little everyday responsibility things that overlap with work-life stuff, like I’ll forget to return phone calls even when I meant to do it right away. Or I had this registration form that I needed to turn in, and it just sat in my bag for days—I kept seeing it and thinking, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then somehow it was still there.\n\nAnd with errands, I’ll be driving home and then realize later I completely forgot to stop where I was supposed to. It’s embarrassing because none of it is huge by itself, but it’s the kind of stuff I usually keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think so, not really. I get frustrated with myself, definitely, like when I realize I forgot something again and then I’m annoyed for the rest of the afternoon. But sad or down… I’m not sure I’d call it that. It’s more like, you know when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there, except it’s with actual things I needed to do. So I feel kind of stupid sometimes, but I haven’t really been thinking of it as sadness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… overwhelmed maybe in the normal way? Like when there are too many little things stacked up and I’m trying to remember them all. But anxious, like panic or something, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be going through my day and then remember something I forgot, and then I get annoyed with myself. And then I’ll start thinking about how I need a better system—my sister has this whole color-coded calendar thing, which I always think I should try, but then I buy the planner and don’t really use it. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, really. Like, I still like the same things when I actually sit down to do them. Sometimes I don’t get around to them, but that’s kind of normal for me, I guess.\n\nI was going to say, I have this book I started and it’s been sitting on my nightstand forever, but that happens with books all the time—I’ll buy one because someone at work says it’s good, and then I read three chapters and forget about it. So I don’t know if that’s “loss of interest” exactly. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. That was, what, about 15 months ago? Around then I started noticing I couldn’t just sort of keep things in my head the way I used to.\n\nI mean, I was already forgetful before, so it’s not brand new. But adding classes after work kind of made everything feel like there were too many tabs open. I’d be trying to remember work stuff, class stuff, and then normal home things, and I’d lose track more often. So… yeah, I guess compared to a year ago, it’s been more noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: Um… learning new information? I don’t know. I guess it depends what kind of information you mean.\n\nLike if someone explains something to me and I’m actually paying attention, I can usually get it. But sometimes if it’s a lot of steps, I’ll write it down and then later I look at my notes and think, “Why did I write it like that?” That happened with this new phone system at work—well, not new-new, but they changed one part of it, and everyone was complaining about it anyway, so maybe that wasn’t just me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I can’t learn things, though. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. But I haven’t really noticed some big problem with learning, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not that I can never focus, but I get pulled off really easily.\n\nLike at the office, if a phone rings or people are talking nearby, suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was typing. Or in class, someone shifts around or whispers and I lose my place. Even just a random thought, like “Did I leave that thing out at home?” can take me completely away from what I was doing. Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.\n\nGuilt… maybe if it affects someone else, I’ll feel bad for a bit. Like if I forget to pass along a message at work—well, I try not to, because I keep sticky notes everywhere, which drives my coworker a little crazy because they end up on the edge of my monitor and then I forget which ones are old. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having big feelings of worthlessness or guilt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or make decisions compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I mean… significant decline, I’m not sure I’d say that. I’m still getting by day to day. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.\n\nBut compared to a few years ago… or maybe I’m just noticing it more now because I’m trying to move up at work—I keep thinking about this Office Manager role that might open up, and then I’ll remember, like, oh, I missed following up on that one email, or I didn’t circle back with someone when I said I would. And with school stuff too, I’ve missed deadlines enough times that it kind of scares me.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s enough that I worry people are going to see me as unreliable, even though I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from your past?\n\nPatient: Trouble recalling memories from my past… um, not really, I don’t think. Like, if you ask me about high school or old jobs or family stuff, I can usually remember it. Sometimes it takes me a second, especially if someone asks out of nowhere, but I don’t think I’ve noticed some big problem with my memory like that.\n\nI mean, I do have random moments where I’ll remember a thing from years ago because I saw a certain kind of notebook or heard a song in the grocery store—actually that happened last week, I heard this song they used to play all the time at this one store I worked near—but no, I wouldn’t say I have trouble recalling my past in a serious way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… a little. I mean, not like people are sitting me down and saying, “You’re ruining things,” but I can tell it affects things.

But sometimes if it’s a lot of steps, I’ll write it down and then later I look at my notes and think, “Why did I write it like that?” That happened with this new phone system at work—well, not new-new, but they changed one part of it, and everyone was complaining about it anyway, so maybe that wasn’t just me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I can’t learn things, though. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. But I haven’t really noticed some big problem with learning, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not that I can never focus, but I get pulled off really easily.\n\nLike at the office, if a phone rings or people are talking nearby, suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was typing. Or in class, someone shifts around or whispers and I lose my place. Even just a random thought, like “Did I leave that thing out at home?” can take me completely away from what I was doing. Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.\n\nGuilt… maybe if it affects someone else, I’ll feel bad for a bit. Like if I forget to pass along a message at work—well, I try not to, because I keep sticky notes everywhere, which drives my coworker a little crazy because they end up on the edge of my monitor and then I forget which ones are old. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having big feelings of worthlessness or guilt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or make decisions compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I mean… significant decline, I’m not sure I’d say that. I’m still getting by day to day. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.\n\nBut compared to a few years ago… or maybe I’m just noticing it more now because I’m trying to move up at work—I keep thinking about this Office Manager role that might open up, and then I’ll remember, like, oh, I missed following up on that one email, or I didn’t circle back with someone when I said I would. And with school stuff too, I’ve missed deadlines enough times that it kind of scares me.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s enough that I worry people are going to see me as unreliable, even though I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from your past?\n\nPatient: Trouble recalling memories from my past… um, not really, I don’t think. Like, if you ask me about high school or old jobs or family stuff, I can usually remember it. Sometimes it takes me a second, especially if someone asks out of nowhere, but I don’t think I’ve noticed some big problem with my memory like that.\n\nI mean, I do have random moments where I’ll remember a thing from years ago because I saw a certain kind of notebook or heard a song in the grocery store—actually that happened last week, I heard this song they used to play all the time at this one store I worked near—but no, I wouldn’t say I have trouble recalling my past in a serious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um… stressful or traumatic? I don’t think there’s anything major like that. I mean, life gets stressful—work gets busy, and sometimes I get behind on things, and then I’ll start thinking about all the little stuff I need to catch up on, like returning a call or mailing something, and somehow I’ll remember I still have laundry in the washer from yesterday… but no, I don’t think I’ve had some big traumatic event recently.\n\nIn the past, nothing is really coming to mind that I’d call trauma. I guess everyone has rough patches, but nothing that feels like, you know, the main reason I’m here or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness or difficulties with daily tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. And then someone’s waiting on me, or I have to explain why it didn’t get done.\n\nLike with family stuff, it’s embarrassing because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then it’s just… gone until later. Yeah, I mean… I’m still getting by at work. I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything, but there have been enough little misses that I’m starting to worry about it.

Like, I’ll forget to follow up on a small thing after a meeting, or I’ll mean to send something and then get pulled into something else—oh, and our office copier has been jamming constantly, which sounds unrelated, but then I’ll go to fix that and forget what I was doing before. So it’s not usually huge disasters, but it adds up.

And I’ve had some school-related deadlines too—forms or deadlines I was supposed to turn in—that I’ve missed or nearly missed. I guess what worries me is people might start thinking I’m unreliable. I’ve been hoping to move toward an Office Manager role eventually, and I don’t want these small slip-ups to make me look like I can’t handle it.

Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, not like people are sitting me down and saying, “You’re ruining things,” but I can tell it affects things.\n\nWhat finally made me ask for help is I keep forgetting these little things I usually handle—like returning calls, or turning in a registration form, or I’ll be driving home and completely forget I was supposed to stop for an errand. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I’m not falling apart or anything, like I still get through the day, but it’s harder than it used to feel.

It’s more these small things piling up—missing a work follow-up after I told myself I’d do it, or realizing a deadline for a school form was closer than I thought, or already passed. And then I start worrying that people are going to see me as unreliable. Which is frustrating, because I do care, and I’m trying.

I’ve been wanting to move toward an Office Manager role eventually, and I guess that’s part of why it bothers me so much. Like if I can’t keep track of these little pieces now, I worry it’s going to hurt my reputation or make it look like I couldn’t handle more responsibility.

It’s more these little everyday responsibility things that overlap with work-life stuff, like I’ll forget to return phone calls even when I meant to do it right away. Or I had this registration form that I needed to turn in, and it just sat in my bag for days—I kept seeing it and thinking, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then somehow it was still there.\n\nAnd with errands, I’ll be driving home and then realize later I completely forgot to stop where I was supposed to. It’s embarrassing because none of it is huge by itself, but it’s the kind of stuff I usually keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think so, not really. It’s more like, you know when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there, except it’s with actual things I needed to do. So… I guess I’m not sure if that counts as overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, really. Like, I still like the same things when I actually sit down to do them. Sometimes I don’t get around to them, but that’s kind of normal for me, I guess.\n\nI was going to say, I have this book I started and it’s been sitting on my nightstand forever, but that happens with books all the time—I’ll buy one because someone at work says it’s good, and then I read three chapters and forget about it. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. That was, what, about 15 months ago? Around then I started noticing I couldn’t just sort of keep things in my head the way I used to.\n\nI mean, I was already forgetful before, so it’s not brand new. But adding classes after work kind of made everything feel like there were too many tabs open. I’d be trying to remember work stuff, class stuff, and then normal home things, and I’d lose track more often. So… yeah, I guess compared to a year ago, it’s been more noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: Um… learning new information? I don’t know. I guess it depends what kind of information you mean.\n\nLike if someone explains something to me and I’m actually paying attention, I can usually get it. But sometimes if it’s a lot of steps, I’ll write it down and then later I look at my notes and think, “Why did I write it like that?” That happened with this new phone system at work—well, not new-new, but they changed one part of it, and everyone was complaining about it anyway, so maybe that wasn’t just me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I can’t learn things, though. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. It’s not that I can never focus, but I get pulled off really easily.\n\nLike at the office, if a phone rings or people are talking nearby, suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was typing. Or in class, someone shifts around or whispers and I lose my place. Even just a random thought, like “Did I leave that thing out at home?” can take me completely away from what I was doing. Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.\n\nGuilt… maybe if it affects someone else, I’ll feel bad for a bit. Like if I forget to pass along a message at work—well, I try not to, because I keep sticky notes everywhere, which drives my coworker a little crazy because they end up on the edge of my monitor and then I forget which ones are old. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having big feelings of worthlessness or guilt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or make decisions compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I mean… significant decline, I’m not sure I’d say that. I’m still getting by day to day. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.\n\nBut compared to a few years ago… or maybe I’m just noticing it more now because I’m trying to move up at work—I keep thinking about this Office Manager role that might open up, and then I’ll remember, like, oh, I missed following up on that one email, or I didn’t circle back with someone when I said I would. Like, if you ask me about high school or old jobs or family stuff, I can usually remember it. Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s been steadily getting worse for years and years, exactly. It’s more like I really started noticing it after I began my evening certificate program, which was… about 15 months ago now, I think.

Sometimes I don’t get around to them, but that’s kind of normal for me, I guess.\n\nI was going to say, I have this book I started and it’s been sitting on my nightstand forever, but that happens with books all the time—I’ll buy one because someone at work says it’s good, and then I read three chapters and forget about it. So I don’t know if that’s “loss of interest” exactly. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. That was, what, about 15 months ago? Around then I started noticing I couldn’t just sort of keep things in my head the way I used to.\n\nI mean, I was already forgetful before, so it’s not brand new. But adding classes after work kind of made everything feel like there were too many tabs open. I’d be trying to remember work stuff, class stuff, and then normal home things, and I’d lose track more often. So… yeah, I guess compared to a year ago, it’s been more noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: Um… learning new information? I don’t know. I guess it depends what kind of information you mean.\n\nLike if someone explains something to me and I’m actually paying attention, I can usually get it. But sometimes if it’s a lot of steps, I’ll write it down and then later I look at my notes and think, “Why did I write it like that?” That happened with this new phone system at work—well, not new-new, but they changed one part of it, and everyone was complaining about it anyway, so maybe that wasn’t just me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I can’t learn things, though. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. But I haven’t really noticed some big problem with learning, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble focusing or concentrating on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not that I can never focus, but I get pulled off really easily.\n\nLike at the office, if a phone rings or people are talking nearby, suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was typing. Or in class, someone shifts around or whispers and I lose my place. Even just a random thought, like “Did I leave that thing out at home?” can take me completely away from what I was doing. Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.\n\nGuilt… maybe if it affects someone else, I’ll feel bad for a bit. Like if I forget to pass along a message at work—well, I try not to, because I keep sticky notes everywhere, which drives my coworker a little crazy because they end up on the edge of my monitor and then I forget which ones are old. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having big feelings of worthlessness or guilt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or make decisions compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I mean… significant decline, I’m not sure I’d say that. I’m still getting by day to day. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.\n\nBut compared to a few years ago… or maybe I’m just noticing it more now because I’m trying to move up at work—I keep thinking about this Office Manager role that might open up, and then I’ll remember, like, oh, I missed following up on that one email, or I didn’t circle back with someone when I said I would. And with school stuff too, I’ve missed deadlines enough times that it kind of scares me.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s enough that I worry people are going to see me as unreliable, even though I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from your past?\n\nPatient: Trouble recalling memories from my past… um, not really, I don’t think. Like, if you ask me about high school or old jobs or family stuff, I can usually remember it. Sometimes it takes me a second, especially if someone asks out of nowhere, but I don’t think I’ve noticed some big problem with my memory like that.\n\nI mean, I do have random moments where I’ll remember a thing from years ago because I saw a certain kind of notebook or heard a song in the grocery store—actually that happened last week, I heard this song they used to play all the time at this one store I worked near—but no, I wouldn’t say I have trouble recalling my past in a serious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um… stressful or traumatic? I don’t think there’s anything major like that. I mean, life gets stressful—work gets busy, and sometimes I get behind on things, and then I’ll start thinking about all the little stuff I need to catch up on, like returning a call or mailing something, and somehow I’ll remember I still have laundry in the washer from yesterday… but no, I don’t think I’ve had some big traumatic event recently.\n\nIn the past, nothing is really coming to mind that I’d call trauma. I guess everyone has rough patches, but nothing that feels like, you know, the main reason I’m here or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness or difficulties with daily tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, not like people are sitting me down and saying, “You’re ruining things,” but I can tell it affects things.\n\nWhat finally made me ask for help is I keep forgetting these little things I usually handle—like returning calls, or turning in a registration form, or I’ll be driving home and completely forget I was supposed to stop for an errand. And then someone’s waiting on me, or I have to explain why it didn’t get done.\n\nLike with family stuff, it’s embarrassing because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then it’s just… gone until later. And then I feel bad, because it probably looks like I’m being flaky.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness or difficulties affecting your job or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’m still getting by at work. I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything, but there have been enough little misses that I’m starting to worry about it.\n\nLike, I’ll forget to follow up on a small thing after a meeting, or I’ll mean to send something and then get pulled into something else—oh, and our office copier has been jamming constantly, which sounds unrelated, but then I’ll go to fix that and forget what I was doing before. So it’s not usually huge disasters, but it adds up.\n\nAnd I’ve had some school-related deadlines too—forms or deadlines I was supposed to turn in—that I’ve missed or nearly missed. I guess what worries me is people might start thinking I’m unreliable. I’ve been hoping to move toward an Office Manager role eventually, and I don’t want these small slip-ups to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to manage your daily life due to these memory and concentration issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m not falling apart or anything, like I still get through the day, but it’s harder than it used to feel.\n\nIt’s more these small things piling up—missing a work follow-up after I told myself I’d do it, or realizing a deadline for a school form was closer than I thought, or already passed. And then I start worrying that people are going to see me as unreliable. Which is frustrating, because I do care, and I’m trying.\n\nI’ve been wanting to move toward an Office Manager role eventually, and I guess that’s part of why it bothers me so much. Like if I can’t keep track of these little pieces now, I worry it’s going to hurt my reputation or make it look like I couldn’t handle more responsibility.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks at home or work due to your memory issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. It’s mostly the little tasks, which is what makes it so annoying. Like returning a call, or turning in some registration form I already knew I had to do, or I’ll be on my way home and I was supposed to stop for something, and then I just… drive straight home.\n\nAnd then later I remember, usually at the worst time. It’s not like I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll even be thinking about it earlier in the day, and then something else comes up—like a text, or I start making dinner, or whatever—and it’s gone. That’s kind of what finally made me ask for help, because these are things I normally handle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and concentration issues have gotten progressively worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s been steadily getting worse for years and years, exactly. It’s more like I really started noticing it after I began my evening certificate program, which was… about 15 months ago now, I think.\n\nBefore that I had my moments, like anyone, but once I added classes after work and all the extra deadlines and paperwork—sorry, I’m thinking about this one form I almost forgot to turn in—it just became a lot harder to keep organized. So yes, it feels worse than it used to, but mainly since taking on that program.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s like my abilities have dropped off compared to when I was younger, exactly. It’s more like… some of this has kind of always been there, now that I think about it.

Even in elementary school I was that kid who forgot permission slips or had homework somewhere in my backpack but couldn’t find it. My mom or my teacher would have to walk me through projects step by step, like, “Okay, do this part next,” because otherwise I’d get lost in it or not finish. I remember my mom signing things at the last minute a lot—actually she used to keep this little spot by the door for school papers, which worked until I forgot to put them there.

So I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve significantly declined from when I was younger. It’s more that the same kind of stuff is showing up now in adult life, where the consequences feel bigger.

It’s more these little everyday responsibility things that overlap with work-life stuff, like I’ll forget to return phone calls even when I meant to do it right away. Or I had this registration form that I needed to turn in, and it just sat in my bag for days—I kept seeing it and thinking, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then somehow it was still there.\n\nAnd with errands, I’ll be driving home and then realize later I completely forgot to stop where I was supposed to. Like, I still like the same things when I actually sit down to do them. Sometimes I don’t get around to them, but that’s kind of normal for me, I guess.\n\nI was going to say, I have this book I started and it’s been sitting on my nightstand forever, but that happens with books all the time—I’ll buy one because someone at work says it’s good, and then I read three chapters and forget about it. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your forgetfulness is worse than it was a year ago?\n\nPatient: Worse than a year ago… maybe not like suddenly worse, but it’s definitely been harder to manage since I started that evening certificate program. I’d be trying to remember work stuff, class stuff, and then normal home things, and I’d lose track more often. I guess it depends what kind of information you mean.\n\nLike if someone explains something to me and I’m actually paying attention, I can usually get it. It’s more… I don’t know, I might need to hear it again or have it in front of me. Then I have to kind of restart and figure out where I was.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually tired… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s tired, right? Especially after work, and then you get home and there’s always something that needs doing—laundry or dishes, and I’ll start one thing and then notice another thing, like the mail piled up on the counter.\n\nBut, um… I’m not sure I’d call it unusual. I haven’t really been thinking of it as a main problem. I do get tired sometimes, but nothing where I’m like, “This is really different” or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt about your forgetfulness or anything else?\n\nPatient: Worthlessness? Um… I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, I get annoyed with myself when I forget something, like anyone would, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need to do better about writing that down,” but I don’t sit there feeling like I’m a terrible person or anything.\n\nGuilt… maybe if it affects someone else, I’ll feel bad for a bit. Like if I forget to pass along a message at work—well, I try not to, because I keep sticky notes everywhere, which drives my coworker a little crazy because they end up on the edge of my monitor and then I forget which ones are old. But no, I don’t think I’ve been having big feelings of worthlessness or guilt like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks or make decisions compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I mean… significant decline, I’m not sure I’d say that. I’m still getting by day to day. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.\n\nBut compared to a few years ago… or maybe I’m just noticing it more now because I’m trying to move up at work—I keep thinking about this Office Manager role that might open up, and then I’ll remember, like, oh, I missed following up on that one email, or I didn’t circle back with someone when I said I would. And with school stuff too, I’ve missed deadlines enough times that it kind of scares me.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s enough that I worry people are going to see me as unreliable, even though I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from your past?\n\nPatient: Trouble recalling memories from my past… um, not really, I don’t think. Like, if you ask me about high school or old jobs or family stuff, I can usually remember it. Sometimes it takes me a second, especially if someone asks out of nowhere, but I don’t think I’ve noticed some big problem with my memory like that.\n\nI mean, I do have random moments where I’ll remember a thing from years ago because I saw a certain kind of notebook or heard a song in the grocery store—actually that happened last week, I heard this song they used to play all the time at this one store I worked near—but no, I wouldn’t say I have trouble recalling my past in a serious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um… stressful or traumatic? I don’t think there’s anything major like that. I mean, life gets stressful—work gets busy, and sometimes I get behind on things, and then I’ll start thinking about all the little stuff I need to catch up on, like returning a call or mailing something, and somehow I’ll remember I still have laundry in the washer from yesterday… but no, I don’t think I’ve had some big traumatic event recently.\n\nIn the past, nothing is really coming to mind that I’d call trauma. And then someone’s waiting on me, or I have to explain why it didn’t get done.\n\nLike with family stuff, it’s embarrassing because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then it’s just… gone until later. I go to work, I get things done, I make decisions when I have to.\n\nBut compared to a few years ago… or maybe I’m just noticing it more now because I’m trying to move up at work—I keep thinking about this Office Manager role that might open up, and then I’ll remember, like, oh, I missed following up on that one email, or I didn’t circle back with someone when I said I would. And with school stuff too, I’ve missed deadlines enough times that it kind of scares me.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s enough that I worry people are going to see me as unreliable, even though I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from your past?\n\nPatient: Trouble recalling memories from my past… um, not really, I don’t think. Like, if you ask me about high school or old jobs or family stuff, I can usually remember it. Sometimes it takes me a second, especially if someone asks out of nowhere, but I don’t think I’ve noticed some big problem with my memory like that.\n\nI mean, I do have random moments where I’ll remember a thing from years ago because I saw a certain kind of notebook or heard a song in the grocery store—actually that happened last week, I heard this song they used to play all the time at this one store I worked near—but no, I wouldn’t say I have trouble recalling my past in a serious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um… stressful or traumatic? I don’t think there’s anything major like that. I mean, life gets stressful—work gets busy, and sometimes I get behind on things, and then I’ll start thinking about all the little stuff I need to catch up on, like returning a call or mailing something, and somehow I’ll remember I still have laundry in the washer from yesterday… but no, I don’t think I’ve had some big traumatic event recently.\n\nIn the past, nothing is really coming to mind that I’d call trauma. I guess everyone has rough patches, but nothing that feels like, you know, the main reason I’m here or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness or difficulties with daily tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, not like people are sitting me down and saying, “You’re ruining things,” but I can tell it affects things.\n\nWhat finally made me ask for help is I keep forgetting these little things I usually handle—like returning calls, or turning in a registration form, or I’ll be driving home and completely forget I was supposed to stop for an errand. And then someone’s waiting on me, or I have to explain why it didn’t get done.\n\nLike with family stuff, it’s embarrassing because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then it’s just… gone until later. And then I feel bad, because it probably looks like I’m being flaky.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness or difficulties affecting your job or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’m still getting by at work. I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything, but there have been enough little misses that I’m starting to worry about it.\n\nLike, I’ll forget to follow up on a small thing after a meeting, or I’ll mean to send something and then get pulled into something else—oh, and our office copier has been jamming constantly, which sounds unrelated, but then I’ll go to fix that and forget what I was doing before. So it’s not usually huge disasters, but it adds up.\n\nAnd I’ve had some school-related deadlines too—forms or deadlines I was supposed to turn in—that I’ve missed or nearly missed. I guess what worries me is people might start thinking I’m unreliable. I’ve been hoping to move toward an Office Manager role eventually, and I don’t want these small slip-ups to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to manage your daily life due to these memory and concentration issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m not falling apart or anything, like I still get through the day, but it’s harder than it used to feel.\n\nIt’s more these small things piling up—missing a work follow-up after I told myself I’d do it, or realizing a deadline for a school form was closer than I thought, or already passed. I don’t think there’s anything major like that. I mean, life gets stressful—work gets busy, and sometimes I get behind on things, and then I’ll start thinking about all the little stuff I need to catch up on, like returning a call or mailing something, and somehow I’ll remember I still have laundry in the washer from yesterday… but no, I don’t think I’ve had some big traumatic event recently.\n\nIn the past, nothing is really coming to mind that I’d call trauma. I guess everyone has rough patches, but nothing that feels like, you know, the main reason I’m here or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness or difficulties with daily tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, not like people are sitting me down and saying, “You’re ruining things,” but I can tell it affects things.\n\nWhat finally made me ask for help is I keep forgetting these little things I usually handle—like returning calls, or turning in a registration form, or I’ll be driving home and completely forget I was supposed to stop for an errand. And then someone’s waiting on me, or I have to explain why it didn’t get done.\n\nLike with family stuff, it’s embarrassing because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll have every intention of doing it, and then it’s just… gone until later. And then I feel bad, because it probably looks like I’m being flaky.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness or difficulties affecting your job or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I’m still getting by at work. I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything, but there have been enough little misses that I’m starting to worry about it.\n\nLike, I’ll forget to follow up on a small thing after a meeting, or I’ll mean to send something and then get pulled into something else—oh, and our office copier has been jamming constantly, which sounds unrelated, but then I’ll go to fix that and forget what I was doing before. So it’s not usually huge disasters, but it adds up.\n\nAnd I’ve had some school-related deadlines too—forms or deadlines I was supposed to turn in—that I’ve missed or nearly missed. I guess what worries me is people might start thinking I’m unreliable. I’ve been hoping to move toward an Office Manager role eventually, and I don’t want these small slip-ups to make me look like I can’t handle it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to manage your daily life due to these memory and concentration issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m not falling apart or anything, like I still get through the day, but it’s harder than it used to feel.\n\nIt’s more these small things piling up—missing a work follow-up after I told myself I’d do it, or realizing a deadline for a school form was closer than I thought, or already passed. And then I start worrying that people are going to see me as unreliable. Which is frustrating, because I do care, and I’m trying.\n\nI’ve been wanting to move toward an Office Manager role eventually, and I guess that’s part of why it bothers me so much. Like if I can’t keep track of these little pieces now, I worry it’s going to hurt my reputation or make it look like I couldn’t handle more responsibility.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks at home or work due to your memory issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I am. It’s mostly the little tasks, which is what makes it so annoying. Like returning a call, or turning in some registration form I already knew I had to do, or I’ll be on my way home and I was supposed to stop for something, and then I just… drive straight home.\n\nAnd then later I remember, usually at the worst time. It’s not like I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll even be thinking about it earlier in the day, and then something else comes up—like a text, or I start making dinner, or whatever—and it’s gone. That’s kind of what finally made me ask for help, because these are things I normally handle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and concentration issues have gotten progressively worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if I’d say it’s been steadily getting worse for years and years, exactly. It’s more like I really started noticing it after I began my evening certificate program, which was… about 15 months ago now, I think.\n\nBefore that I had my moments, like anyone, but once I added classes after work and all the extra deadlines and paperwork—sorry, I’m thinking about this one form I almost forgot to turn in—it just became a lot harder to keep organized. So yes, it feels worse than it used to, but mainly since taking on that program.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your overall mental abilities, such as memory, concentration, and decision-making, have declined significantly compared to when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like my abilities have dropped off compared to when I was younger, exactly. It’s more like… some of this has kind of always been there, now that I think about it.\n\nEven in elementary school I was that kid who forgot permission slips or had homework somewhere in my backpack but couldn’t find it. My mom or my teacher would have to walk me through projects step by step, like, “Okay, do this part next,” because otherwise I’d get lost in it or not finish. I remember my mom signing things at the last minute a lot—actually she used to keep this little spot by the door for school papers, which worked until I forgot to put them there.\n\nSo I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve significantly declined from when I was younger. It’s more that the same kind of stuff is showing up now in adult life, where the consequences feel bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your forgetfulness affecting your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. Not in a huge dramatic way, but I’ll forget to return calls, and then I feel bad because it looks like I’m ignoring people. Or I’ll mean to take care of something, like turning in a registration form or stopping for an errand on the way home, and then I just don’t do it.\n\nIt’s frustrating because these are little things I normally handle, you know? And then later I’m like, “Oh my gosh, I forgot again.” That’s really what pushed me to ask for help.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
